No Invariant Perfect Qubit Codes
Refik Mansuroglu, Hanno Sahlmann

Abstract
Perfect tensors describe highly entangled quantum states that have attracted particular attention in the fields of quantum information theory and quantum gravity. In loop quantum gravity, the natural question arises whether SU(2) invariant tensors, which are fundamental ingredients of the basis states of spacetime, can also be perfect. In this work, we present a number of general constraints for the layout of such invariant perfect tensors (IPTs) and further describe a systematic and constructive approach to check the existence of an IPT of given valence. We apply our algorithm to show that no qubit encoding of valence 6 can be described by an IPT and close a gap to prove a no-go theorem for invariant perfect qubit encodings. We also provide two alternative proofs for the non-existence of 4-valent qubit IPTs which has been shown in Higuchi and Sudbery 2000 ; Li et al. 2017 .



I
Introduction

Perfect tensors are powerful structures that encode highly entangled states of many-body quantum systems. Their defining property is that any reduced density matrix of at most half of the system is maximally mixed. As such they describe
absolutely maximally entangled
states
Helwig et al. 2012
.
Perfect tensors have been studied in a broad spectrum of fields. A system of qubits, for instance, can be encoded by a perfect tensor with maximal code distance, in the context of quantum error correction. Perfect tensors have also been shown to reproduce features of the AdS/CFT correspondence
Pastawski et al. 2015
;
Bhattacharyya et al. 2016
and illustrate the Ryu-Takayanagi formula
Ryu and Takayanagi 2006
emerging from many-body entanglement. This way, erasure of quantum information can be reconstructed and a quantum error correcting code can be built
Almheiri et al. 2015
;
Cao and Lackey 2022
. Random tensor networks with large bond dimension indicate similar features
Hayden et al. 2016
.
What makes perfect tensors particularly interesting in the context of holographic duality is the fact that they represent quantum channels of maximal chaos
Hosur et al. 2016
making them suitable candidates for representing the holographic dual to the bulk quantum state of a black hole
Maldacena et al. 2016
. In all of these applications, perfect tensors appear as components of a tensor network.

From the perspective of quantum gravity, holography and the entanglement/geometry correspondence are two important open questions
de Boer et al. 2022
, and the models referred to contain very important insights. At the same time, they are somewhat ad hoc. It is therefore important to note that tensor networks also play an important role in the definition of quantum states in a quantum theory of gravity derived from first principles. In loop quantum gravity
Thiemann 2007
;
Ashtekar and Singh 2011
, special tensor networks represent basis states that diagonalize certain aspects of spatial geometry. As such, the tensors of these
spin networks

Penrose 1971a
;
Baez 1996
represent quantum states of geometric polyhedra
Bianchi et al. 2011
.

The bond dimensions in spin networks are not necessarily uniform. They corresponds to geometric areas of the faces of polyhedra dual to the network. While we will mostly consider tensors with physical dimension 2 -- corresponding to spin 1/2 -- in the present work, higher and possibly non-uniform dimensions would also be physically relevant.
1
1

1

While in the path integral approach to LQG, the limit of large dimensions is often associated with the classical limit
Barrett et al. 2010
, this is not unequivocal. In the canonical approach, low spins play an important role. They make up the bulk of the states in the state counting for black hole entropy
Ashtekar et al. 2000
, for example, with large spins exponentially suppressed
Domagala and Lewandowski 2004
.

Spin networks and their linear combinations have been studied under the aspect of entanglement entropy
Bianchi et al. 2019
and holography
Han and Hung 2017
;
Colafranceschi et al. 2022a
;
Colafranceschi et al. 2022b
. It has been shown in
Han and Hung 2017
that the area operator of loop quantum gravity can be related to holographic entanglement entropy, thus recovering the Ryu-Takayanagi formula.

The limits to the existence of perfect tensors have been investigated in the absence of additional symmetries
Scott 2004
;
Higuchi and Sudbery 2000
;
Gisin and Bechmann-Pasquinucci 1998
(see also
Huber et al. 2018
;
Huber and Wyderka 2018
for an illustrative overview). Spin network states are more restrictive in a sense that they come with additional constraints on the tensor network states describing quantum geometry.
The tensors of the spin networks have to be invariant under an action of SU(2) on all tensor factors. In the geometric picture, this invariance corresponds to the closure of the quantized polyhedra. Hence, a pressing question is the existence of invariant perfect tensors (IPTs). This would allow to study geometric correlates of perfectness and give the models for AdS/CFT a geometric interpretation using more than the the network topology. Previous work has investigated IPTs for qudits (arbitrary local dimension
d
) and showed that every 3-valent Wigner 3j-symbol is an IPT
Li et al. 2017
. Apart from that, no more IPTs have been found. In fact, it has been shown that there are no 4-valent IPTs
Higuchi and Sudbery 2000
;
Li et al. 2017
, but for valence
n\geq 5
random invariant tensors are concentrated around perfect tensors
Li et al. 2018
.

In this work, we introduce a systematic method to find IPTs using a decomposition into special coupling schemes that we call bridge states. For the derivation we make use of Penrose’s binor calculus
Penrose 1971b
;
Kauffman 2002
, a graphical notation which is particularly useful when dealing with spin network states
Pietri 1997
;
Mansuroglu and Sahlmann 2021
. We apply the proposed algorithm to give an additional proof for non-existence of 4-valent IPTs for qubit encoding, thereby reproducing a result of
Li et al. 2017
. We also apply the algorithm to 6-valent qubit encodings and show that also those hexagonal invariant perfect structures are forbidden. We conclude with a no-go theorem for arbitrary qubit encodings making use of the Scott bound
Scott 2004
. Moreover, we put general constraints on the layout of IPTs with even valence: All indices are restricted to correspond to the same spin representation. A weaker restriction also holds for odd valence.

We structure the paper as follows. After defining the important notions, we use representation theory arguments to put restrictions to the layout of general IPTs and use that to prove (again, see
Li et al. 2017
) a no-go theorem for 4-valent qubit in section
III
. In section
IV
, we derive a master equation for the coefficients determining an IPT of given valence and solve it for valence four and six in section
V
. With this, we present a second proof for the no-go theorem of 4-valent qubit encodings and a new no-go theorem of 6-valent qubit encodings which imply a no-go theorem for generic valence.

Note added in proof
: After publication of this work as a preprint and submission to JHEP, an independent work that proves the non-existence of perfect SU(2) invariant qubit codes
Bernards 2022
has been brought to our attention. Meanwhile it is available as a pre-print
Bernards and Gühne 2022
. The work rules out the perfectness of Werner states, i.e., qubit codes that are
U(d)
invariant up to phase, where
d
is the local dimension.
U(d)
invariance up to phase is a stronger assumption for higher
d
, but for qubits their statements coincide with our finding that there are no SU(2) invariant perfect codes.

II
Basic notions and Concepts

Let us start with the introduction of conventions and notions we need for the further study. We freely switch between considering the same object
I
as a linear map
I:V_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{k}\rightarrow V_{k+1}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{n}
, a state in
V_{1}^{*}\otimes\ldots\otimes V^{*}_{k}\otimes V_{k+1}\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{n}
and its tensor representation
I^{a_{1}\ldots a_{m}}{}_{a_{k+1}\ldots a_{n}}
. We will furthermore not distinguish between
V
and
V^{*}
and pull indices with
\delta
.

All vector spaces will carry representations of
SU(2)
. We denote by
j
both, a spin label
j\in\mathbb{N}/2
and the corresponding
2j+1
-dimensional irrep of
SU(2)
.

The first important notion is that of an invariant tensor, also denoted intertwiner or equivariant map in the literature.

Definition II.1
.

A tensor
I:j_{1}\otimes...\otimes j_{k}\to j_{k+1}\otimes...\otimes j_{n}
is called invariant, if it satisfies

Note that invariant tensors
I
exist if and only if a coupling scheme to the trivial representation
j=0
exists, i.e.
I:j_{1}\otimes...\otimes j_{n}\to 0
. Next, we define the notion of bipartition of tensors.

Definition II.2
.

Let
I
be any tensor with coefficients
\mathchoice{I^{{{a_{1}...a_{k}}\mathchoice{\makebox[30.8297pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[30.8297pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.79448pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[15.42612pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[25.7194pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.7194pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[13.9458pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[11.29903pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b_{k+1}...b_{n}}}}}{I^{{{a_{1}...a_{k}}\mathchoice{\makebox[30.8297pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[30.8297pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.79448pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[15.42612pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[25.7194pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.7194pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[13.9458pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[11.29903pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b_{k+1}...b_{n}}}}}{I^{{{a_{1}...a_{k}}\mathchoice{\makebox[30.8297pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[30.8297pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.79448pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[15.42612pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[25.7194pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.7194pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[13.9458pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[11.29903pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b_{k+1}...b_{n}}}}}{I^{{{a_{1}...a_{k}}\mathchoice{\makebox[30.8297pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[30.8297pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.79448pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[15.42612pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[25.7194pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.7194pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[13.9458pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[11.29903pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b_{k+1}...b_{n}}}}}
and
\mathcal{I}=\{1,...,n\}
its index set. A bipartition is a pair
(A,B)
of ordered tuples

, such that as sets

.

\displaystyle\mathchoice{(JI)^{{{a_{1}...a_{k}}\mathchoice{\makebox[30.94386pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[30.94386pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.82967pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[15.45123pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[25.7194pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.7194pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[13.9458pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[11.29903pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c_{k+1}...c_{n}}}}}{(JI)^{{{a_{1}...a_{k}}\mathchoice{\makebox[30.94386pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[30.94386pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.82967pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[15.45123pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[25.7194pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.7194pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[13.9458pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[11.29903pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c_{k+1}...c_{n}}}}}{(JI)^{{{a_{1}...a_{k}}\mathchoice{\makebox[30.94386pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[30.94386pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.82967pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[15.45123pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[25.7194pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.7194pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[13.9458pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[11.29903pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c_{k+1}...c_{n}}}}}{(JI)^{{{a_{1}...a_{k}}\mathchoice{\makebox[30.94386pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[30.94386pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.82967pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[15.45123pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[25.7194pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.7194pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[13.9458pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[11.29903pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c_{k+1}...c_{n}}}}}=\mathchoice{I^{{{a_{1}...a_{k}}\mathchoice{\makebox[30.8297pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[30.8297pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.79448pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[15.42612pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[25.7194pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[25.7194pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[13.9458pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1 ptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.60307pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c_{k+1}...c_{n}}}}}{J^{{{b_{1}...b_{k}}\mathchoice{\makebox[30.94386pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[30.94386pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.82967pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[15.45123pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[24.07744pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[24.07744pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.97144pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.60307pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c_{k+1}...c_{n}}}}}{J^{{{b_{1}...b_{k}}\mathchoice{\makebox[30.94386pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[30.94386pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[17.82967pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[15.45123pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[24.07744pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[24.07744pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.97144pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.60307pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c_{k+1}...c_{n}}}}}

(II.2)

Hence, a bipartition can be thought of as the distinction between input and output of the tensor interpreted as a multilinear function.
If a tensor is a partial isometry in all possible bipartitions that allow an embedding from input to output, i.e.
|A|\leq|B|
, or formally:

Definition II.3
.

A tensor
I:j_{1}\otimes...\otimes j_{n}\rightarrow 0
is called perfect, if for any bipartition
(A,B)
with
|A|\leq|B|
there exists
\lambda\in\mathds{R}
, s.t.
I^{\dagger}I=\lambda\mathds{1}
.

III
Necessary Conditions on Invariant Perfect Tensors

The requirement of perfectness puts a number of restrictions on invariant tensors. For every bipartition to be a partial isometry, we can study the action of an invariant tensor on irreducible subspaces of SU(2) using Schur’s Lemma repeatedly.

Lemma III.1
.

Let
I:j_{1}\otimes...\otimes j_{n}\rightarrow 0
be a non-trivial,
n
-valent invariant, perfect tensor. For any bipartition
(A,B)
with
|A|\leq|B|
, the following statements hold

1.

I
maps irreducible spin
j
subspaces to spin
j
subspaces.

2.

I\rvert_{j}=\mu_{j}\mathds{1}
with
\mu_{j}\in\mathds{C}
and
|\mu_{j}|^{2}=|\mu_{j^{\prime}}|^{2}=\lambda(A)\quad\forall j,j^{\prime}

These statements are depicted in the following diagram that shows the mapping of
I
reduced to the irreducible subspaces.

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\sum_{i\in A}j_{i}&\xrightarrow{\mu_{j_{max}}}&\sum_{i\in B}j_{i}\\
\sum_{i\in A}j_{i}-1&\xrightarrow{\mu_{j_{max}-1}}&\sum_{i\in B}j_{i}-1\\
&\qquad\qquad&\\
...&&...\\
&&\\
\min_{\vec{k}}|\sum_{i\in A}(-1)^{k_{i}}j_{i}|&\xrightarrow{\mu_{j_{min}}}&\min_{\vec{k}}|\sum_{i\in A}(-1)^{k_{i}}j_{i}|\end{matrix}

(III.1)

Proof.

1.

This is a direct consequence of Schur’s lemma.

2.

Also by Schur’s lemma, the action of
I
reduced to an irreducible subrepresentation labelled by a spin quantum number
j
is equal to
\mu_{j}\mathds{1}
with
\mu_{j}\in\mathds{C}
.

For perfectness, we need
I^{\dagger}I=\lambda(A)\mathds{1}
for every bipartition
\mathcal{I}=A\uplus B
, which translated into
|\mu_{j}|^{2}=|\mu_{j^{\prime}}|^{2}=:\lambda\,\forall j,j^{\prime}
and for any bipartition
\mathcal{I}=A\uplus B
.

∎

From the
SU(2)
invariance of
I
, we have assured that every spin
j
subspace in the domain is also found in the image of
I
as long as
|A|\leq|B|
. If we consider the case of even valence
2n
and the balanced bipartitions, i.e.
|A|=|B|
, we can derive limitations on the spin quantum number.

Lemma III.2
.

Let
I
be an invariant, perfect tensor with even valence
2n
. Then all ingoing spins are the same, i.e.
I:j\otimes...\otimes j\to 0

Proof.

We will apply Lemma
III.1
repeatedly for all balanced bipartitions, i.e.
|A|=|B|
. Since, we need to map the maximally coupled spin onto a subspace of equal dimension, we can infer the inequality

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in A}j_{i}\leq\sum_{i\in B}j_{i},

(III.2)

for a fixed bipartition
A,B
. As we consider a balanced bipartition, we can also look at the bipartition, where
A
and
B
are swapped. This makes the inequality an equality

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in A}j_{i}=\sum_{i\in B}j_{i}.

(III.3)

Additionally, we can permute indices through
A
and
B
and get the same equation (
III.3
). One can easily show by induction that (
III.3
) implies
j_{i}=j_{k}\forall i,k\in\mathcal{I}
.
∎

We can also make a statement about the spin quantum numbers of invariant, perfect tensors of odd valence
2n+1
which is, however, less restrictive.

Lemma III.3
.

Let
I
be an invariant, perfect tensor with odd valence
2n+1
. Then the spin quantum numbers of the ingoing edges fulfill

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}j_{i}\leq\sum_{i=n+1}^{2n+1}j_{i}

(III.4)

where we sorted the spins in decreasing order, i.e.
j_{1}\geq...\geq j_{2n+1}
for all bipartitions
\mathcal{I}=A\uplus B
, with
|A|=n
and
|B|=n+1
.

Proof.

The proposition is a direct application of Lemma
III.1
with the bipartition where
A
contains the
n
largest spins.
∎

Note that (
III.4
) also implies inequalities of the same form but with arbitrary permutations of indices in
A
and
B
, as long as
|A|+1=|B|
. We conclude the section with a number of examples and remarks.

Example III.4
.

1.

The invariant tensor
I:\frac{1}{2}\otimes\frac{1}{2}\otimes\frac{1}{2}\otimes\frac{1}{2}\otimes 2\to 0
cannot be perfect by Lemma
III.3
.

2.

The HaPPY code
Pastawski et al. 2015
is constructed from a perfect tensor with valence
n=6
. However, it is not invariant.

3.

If an invariant, perfect tensor encodes qubits only (
j_{i}=\frac{1}{2}\,\forall i
), it needs to have even valence.

Vice versa if an invariant, perfect tensor has even valence and shall encode at least one qubit, i.e.
j_{i}=\frac{1}{2}
for one
i
, then
j_{k}=\frac{1}{2}\,\forall k
.

We want to apply Lemma
III.2
to the special case
n=4
and
j=\frac{1}{2}
and reproduce a result of
Li et al. 2017
with a different proof.

Theorem III.5
.

There are no non-trivial invariant, perfect tensors
I
with valence
n=4
and
j=\frac{1}{2}
.

Proof by contradiction (Sketch).

By Lemma
III.1
, we know that the eigenvalues of an invariant, perfect tensor
I
can only differ by a phase, i.e.
I
takes the form

\displaystyle I^{abcd}=\sqrt{\lambda}(e^{i\phi_{s}}\epsilon^{a(c}\epsilon^{d)b}+e^{i\phi_{a}}\epsilon^{a[c}\epsilon^{d]b})

(III.5)

for the bipartition
(ab)\to(cd)
. Repeating this argument for the repartitions
(ad)\to(cb)
and
(ac)\to(bd)
gives us similar expressions for
I
with potentially different phases. Comparing the three versions of (
III.5
) yields two contradicting constraints for the phase difference
\Delta\phi=\phi_{a}-\phi_{s}
.
∎

See appendix
A
for the full proof. While the arguments of Theorem
III.5
can be generalized to higher valences and spins, it quickly gets combinatorically complicated. We will present a simpler systematic approach to get sufficient conditions for invariant perfect tensors.

IV
Master Equation for Invariant Perfect Tensors

A more down to earth approach to formulate sufficient conditions for an invariant, perfect tensor is achieved by decomposing tensor networks into
bridge states
, coupling schemes
2
2

2

For more information on SU(2) coupling schemes see for example
Wigner 1993
;
Brunnemann and Thiemann 2006
.
that additionally contain the information of a bipartition. The following discussion is focused on a tensor of even valence and
j=\frac{1}{2}
, but it can be readily generalized to higher spins and odd valence. The only change is increase of combinatorial complexity (see Remark
B.7
for a sketch). Let us start by defining bridge states

Definition IV.1

(Bridge State)
.

Let
n\in\mathds{N}
and
(j_{a})_{a\in\{1,...,n_{1}\}},(k_{b})_{b\in\{1,...,n_{2}\}}
lists of spin quantum numbers where
n_{1}+n_{2}=n
and
j_{n_{1}}=k_{n_{2}}
. We define the SU(2) invariant bridge state associated with
j
and
k
as the following coupling scheme

\displaystyle\curlyvee_{(j,k)}=\frac{1}{2}^{\otimes n_{1}}\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{35.45534pt}{9.07634pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{67}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{46.06963pt}{7.9087pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{67}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}}}}}\frac{1}{2}^{\otimes n_{2}}

(IV.1)

Since an invariant coupling exists only for certain data
n_{1},n_{2},j
and
k
, bridge states exist only for certain spin data as well. Implicit in (
IV.1
) is a certain normalisation. For details see the Appendix
B
.

We can directly infer a number of helpful relations between bridge states and invariant tensors

Remark IV.2
.

1.

The bridge state
\curlyvee_{(j,k)}
exists, if and only if
k_{1}=j_{1}=\frac{1}{2}
and
j_{a}=|j_{a-1}\pm\frac{1}{2}|\,\forall a
, and analogously for
k_{a}

2.

Every bridge state implicitly encodes a bipartition of indices. For valence
2n
and
n_{1}=n_{2}=n
, for instance, we get the balanced bipartition (cf. section
III
)

3.

As every bridge state is also a coupling scheme
Wigner 1993
, the set of invariant bridge states for fixed valence and bipartition
n_{1},n_{2}
represents a basis on the space of invariant tensors.

With this, we can decompose any invariant tensor
I
into bridge states
\curlyvee_{(j,k)}
in which a bipartition is implicitly fixed in the ordering of
(j,k)

\displaystyle I=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\\
j_{n_{1}}=k_{n_{2}}\end{subarray}}c_{j,k}\curlyvee_{(j,k)}.

(IV.2)

In the following, we will drop the addendum
j_{n_{1}}=k_{n_{2}}
and assume the coefficients
c_{j,k}
to be zero if this is not satisfied. To derive a necessary and sufficient condition for perfectness of
I
, we need to translate the isometry relation

\displaystyle I^{\dagger}I=\lambda\mathds{1}

(IV.3)

into a set of equations for the coefficients
c_{j,k}
. To write down those equations, we still need the notion of theta networks

Definition IV.3

(Theta Network)
.

Let
2n\in 2\mathds{N}

and
(j_{a}),(k_{b}),\,a,b\in\{1,...,n\}
lists of spin quantum numbers where
j_{1}=k_{1}
and
j_{n}=k_{n}
. The theta-network associated with
j
and
k
is defined as

{}_{j}\Theta_{k}=\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{
\hbox to27.57pt{\vbox to17.87pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 13.40224pt\lower-8.93582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 7.87 0 C 7.87 6.52 4.35 11.81 0 11.81 C -4.35 11.81 -7.87 6.52 -7.87 0 C -7.87 -6.52 -4.35 -11.81 0 -11.81 C 4.35 -11.81 7.87 -6.52 7.87 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.81 0 M 11.81 0 C 11.81 0 11.81 0 11.81 0 C 11.81 0 11.81 0 11.81 0 C 11.81 0 11.81 0 11.81 0 C 11.81 0 11.81 0 11.81 0 Z M 11.81 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.23593pt}{-3.47221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{67}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}}}}},

(IV.4)

where the inner edges all carry spin
\frac{1}{2}
.

Since the theta network has no open ends, it evaluates to a number, and we will denote this number as
{}_{j}\Theta_{k}
in the following.

Theorem IV.4
.

Let
I
be an invariant tensor and
c_{j,k}
coefficients denoting its bridge state decomposition for a fixed bipartition via (
IV.2
).
I
is a perfect tensor, if and only if for every bipartition
A
the coefficients satisfy

\displaystyle\sum_{k}c_{j,k}\overline{c_{j^{\prime},k}}\hskip 5.0pt{}_{k}\Theta_{k}=\lambda(A)\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}\frac{(2j_{n_{1}}+1)^{2}}{{}_{j}\Theta_{j}},

(IV.5)

where
\lambda(A)\in\mathds{R}
might be different for each bipartition
A
.

Proof (Sketch).

The equations (
IV.5
) result from a comparison of coefficients of the two sides of (
IV.3
). The left hand side requires the contraction of
I
with its own adjoint
I^{\dagger}
, which can be calculated using orthogonality relations of bridge states and identities from the binor calculus
Kauffman 2002
(cf. appendix
B
). For the right hand side, a decomposition of the identity operator
\mathds{1}
into bridge states is done (cf. Lemma
B.6
).
∎

For a detailed proof, see appendix
B
. We can directly solve for one of the coefficients from (
IV.5
) for general valence.

Corollary IV.5
.

Let
I
be an invariant partial isometry of valence
2n
and
c_{j,k}
the coefficients of its bridge state decomposition. It holds

\displaystyle|c_{j_{max},j_{max}}|^{2}=\lambda,

(IV.6)

where
j_{max}=\left(\frac{1}{2},1,...,\frac{n}{2}\right)
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of (
IV.5
), when choosing
j=j^{\prime}=j_{max}
.

\displaystyle\sum_{k}c_{j_{max},k}\overline{c_{j_{max},k}}\hskip 5.0pt{}_{k}\Theta_{k}=\lambda\frac{(2j_{n}+1)^{2}}{{}_{j_{max}}\Theta_{j_{max}}},

(IV.7)

In order for
I
to be invariant, only one addend with
k=j_{max}
remains on the left hand side. Finally, using
{}_{j_{max}}\Theta_{j_{max}}=2j_{n}+1
yields the statement.
∎

Since (
IV.5
) has to hold for every possible bipartition, it remains to study how these equations transform under a change of bipartition, a repartition. The repartitioned tensor
\tilde{I}
also can be decomposed into bridge states with new coefficients
\tilde{c}_{j,k}
that, again, have to satisfy (
IV.5
).

However, the master equation (
IV.5
) only needs to be solved once. Since the new coefficients of the repartitioned tensor can be related to the old coefficients by a linear operator
R

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})}=\mathchoice{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[21.70837pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[21.70837pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[13.10074pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.19337pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{(j,k)}}}_{{{(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})}\mathchoice{\makebox[17.58284pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.58284pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.95352pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10172pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[21.70837pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[21.70837pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[13.10074pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.19337pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{(j,k)}}}_{{{(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})}\mathchoice{\makebox[17.58284pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.58284pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.95352pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10172pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[21.70837pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[21.70837pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[13.10074pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.19337pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{(j,k)}}}_{{{(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})}\mathchoice{\makebox[17.58284pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.58284pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.95352pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10172pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[21.70837pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[21.70837pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[13.10074pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.19337pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{(j,k)}}}_{{{(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})}\mathchoice{\makebox[17.58284pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.58284pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.95352pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10172pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}c_{(j,k)},

(IV.8)

it only remains to check consistency of the solution of the master equation (
IV.5
) under every such transformation. In particular, every repartition
R
that does not change the balance between the index sets
A,B
can be written as a series of
r
permutations of neighboring indices

\displaystyle R=P_{i_{r},i_{r}+1}\cdots P_{i_{1},i_{1}+1}.

(IV.9)

Note that the
\{P_{i,i+1}\}
do not necessarily commute. To check (
IV.5
) for every possible bipartition hence requires the exploration of the whole algebra generated by
\{P_{i,i+1}\}
, in general. We show in appendix
C
that some repartitions are trivial in the sense that, if
I
is an IPT then also the repartitioned tensor
\tilde{I}
is an IPT. In particular, every unbalancing repartition which moves an index from the domain into the image space is trivial (cf. Lemma
C.1
). Also balanced repartitions that do not mix indices of the domain or image are trivial (cf. Lemma
C.2
).

It thus remains to investigate the permutation of the neighboring indices across the bridge
P^{*}
(cf. Lemma
C.4
)

I

(IV.10)

and all combinations of trivial repartitions with
P^{*}
. We summarize the systematics in the following algorithm

Algorithm IV.6

(Finding an IPT)
.

For a given valence
2n
the set of IPTs encoding qubits (
j=\frac{1}{2}
) can be found the following way

1.

Solve the master equations (
IV.5
) for one specific bipartition and parameterize the general solution into free amplitudes
A_{1},A_{2},...
and phases
\phi_{1},\phi_{2},...

2.

Calculate the basis transformation
P^{*}
that yields the new coefficients
\tilde{c}
corresponding to the repartitioned tensor which has the neighboring indices flipped across the bridge

3.

Parameterize the
\tilde{c}
the same way as in 1. using new, independent parameters
\tilde{A}_{1},\tilde{A}_{2},...,\tilde{\phi}_{1},\tilde{\phi}_{2},...

4.

Implement constraints on said parameters by identifying
\tilde{c}=P^{*}c

5.

Repeat 2. – 4. with a repartition exchanging the last index in the domain with the second last index (
P_{n+1,n+2}P^{*}P_{n+1,n+2}
), third last index (
P_{n+2,n+3}P_{n+1,n+2}P^{*}P_{n+1,n+2}P_{n+2,n+3}
) etc.

6.

Repeat 2. – 4. with the mirroring repartition that exchanges all domain and image indices

In the end, Algorithm
IV.6
requires at most
n+1
repartitions. If the solution set becomes empty or only contains trivial solutions before every repartition has been checked, the algorithm terminates as no IPT exists.

Note that with Algorithm
IV.6
, it is trivial to see that every 3-valent invariant tensor is also perfect. Since the tensor is unique there is no mixing of basis states. The perfectness relation can be calculated in two lines with the binor calculus (cf. Lemma
B.1
for spin
\frac{1}{2}
case).

V
Two No-Go Theorems for valence Four and Six

We have already shown in Theorem
III.5
, that there are no invariant perfect tensors with valence
2n=4
and spin
j=\frac{1}{2}
. This can also be shown in the bridge state decomposition using Algorithm
IV.6
. We present the full proof in appendix
D
and sketch the most important arguments here.

Second Proof for Theorem
III.5
(Sketch).

Assume we had found an IPT
I
. For a fixed bipartition, we can fix the coefficients of the bridge state decomposition of
I
up to two phases (one relative phase)

\displaystyle|c_{0}|^{2}=\frac{\lambda}{4},\qquad|c_{1}|^{2}=\lambda.

(V.1)

While the mirroring repartition is trivial, the repartition
P^{*}
fixes the relative phase to
\Delta\phi=0
. The last repartition
P_{3,4}P^{*}P_{3,4}
, however, would fix the relative phase of the initial coefficients to
\Delta\phi=\pi
, a contradiction.
∎

Note that 4-valent perfect tensors have been already ruled out without imposing SU(2) invariance in
Higuchi and Sudbery 2000
. One is tempted to go one step further and study the second smallest non-trivial tensor for spin
\frac{1}{2}
particles which is a 6-valent tensor. We will show in an analogous way that also those hexagonal structures cannot be perfect and
SU(2)
invariant at the same time. As before, a detailed proof is presented in appendix
E
and a sketch of the proof is given here.

Theorem V.1
.

There are no non-trivial invariant, perfect tensors
I
with valence
2n=6
and
j=\frac{1}{2}
.

Proof (Sketch).

Assume we had found an IPT
I
. For a fixed bipartition, the master equation (
IV.5
) is solved leaving one free amplitude
A\in\mathds{R}^{+}
and four phases
\phi,\rho,\chi,\psi\in[0,2\pi)

\displaystyle c_{(1,\frac{3}{2},1)}=\sqrt{\lambda}e^{i\phi}\quad c_{(1,\frac{1}{2},1)}=Ae^{i\rho}\quad c_{(1,\frac{1}{2},0)}=\sqrt{\frac{\lambda}{3}-\frac{3}{4}A^{2}}\,e^{i\chi}\quad c_{(0,\frac{1}{2},0)}=\frac{3}{4}Ae^{i\psi}\quad c_{(0,\frac{1}{2},1)}=\sqrt{\frac{\lambda}{3}-\frac{3}{4}A^{2}}e^{i(\rho+\psi-\chi+\pi)}

(V.2)

The repartition
P^{*}
reduces the degrees of freedom by fixing
A=\frac{2}{3}\sqrt{\lambda}
and
\rho=\phi
. Furthermore, the phase
\chi
does not appear anymore, since the coefficients of asymmetric bridge states are necessarily zero
c_{(0,\frac{1}{2},1)}=c_{(1,\frac{1}{2},0)}=0
. Finally, the repartition acting on the second last index on the right hand side
P_{4,5}P^{*}P_{4,5}
projects the solution space to
\lambda=0
which is a trivial solution.
∎

Finally, we can use Theorem
V.1
to close a gap and deduce a no-go theorem for invariant perfect qubit encodings.

Corollary V.2
.

There are no invariant perfect qubit (
j=\frac{1}{2}
) encodings.

Proof.

The statement is a consequence of previous results. A 2-valent IPT is trivially and uniquely given by the identity
I=\mathds{1}
. From invariance, the valence is restricted to even
n
. Valence four is prohibited by Theorem
III.5
and valence six by Theorem
V.1
. Finally, the Scott bound for perfect tensors
Scott 2004
gives the upper bound

\displaystyle n\leq 2(d^{2}-1)

(V.3)

for the valence,
with
d
being the local index dimension. With
d=2
for qubits, we have
n\leq 6
excluding any other valence.
∎

VI
Conclusion

This paper presents a methodology that allows the systematic search for invariant perfect tensors. We restrict the spin quantum numbers of tensors with even and odd valence separately. While for even valent IPTs all indices must necessarily be in the same irreducible representation, for odd valence we prove a weaker condition (
III.4
) that restricts the spin quantum numbers to be not too far away from each other.

We further derive a master equation to determine the coefficients of an IPT decomposed into specific coupling schemes, the bridge states. Following our algorithm, we provide two proofs for the non-existence of 4-valent and one proof for 6-valent IPTs. Combining our results with the Scott bound for perfect tensors, we finally show that there can be no IPTs with qubit indices only.

Several generalizations of our methods to higher spin representations and odd valence leave us with open questions. While the generalization of the master equations (
IV.5
) are straightforward, finding solutions quickly becomes more complex and technical. It thus remains to be shown how further general identities and symmetries can be translated into our framework and exploited to simplify Algorithm
IV.6
.

Another open question that we leave for future work is the study of an alternative definition of IPTs. Instead of allowing at most half of the
indices/particles
on the input side of any bipartition, one might ask for more and allow also bipartitions for which the dimension of the input representation space is smaller or equal to the output. This still allows for an isometric embedding as asked for in the definition of perfect tensors. Although, more bipartitions have to satisfy the isometry relation, the restrictions from section
III
do not generalize straightforwardly. Moreover, if every index has the same local dimension
d
, the two definitions coincide. It remains to be shown how the alternative definitions relate, in general.

We thank Otfried Gühne and Fabian Bernards for discussions, as well as the anonymous referees for their feedback to improve the presentation of this paper. This work is part of the Munich Quantum Valley, which is supported by the Bavarian state government with funds from the Hightech Agenda Bayern Plus.

Appendix A
No 4-valent invariant perfect tensors – Proof I

Proof of Theorem
III.5
.

We proof the statement by contradiction. If there was an invariant, perfect tensor
I
, we knew from Lemma
III.2
that all spin quantum numbers are equal. For a fixed balanced bipartition
|A|=|B|
, the tensor hence maps the following irreducible subrepresentations

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}1&\xrightarrow{\mu_{1}}&1\\
0&\xrightarrow{\mu_{0}}&0\end{matrix}

(A.1)

where
|\mu_{0}|=|\mu_{1}|
. This gives a restriction to
I

\displaystyle I^{abcd}=\sqrt{\lambda}(e^{i\phi_{s}}\epsilon^{a(c}\epsilon^{d)b}+e^{i\phi_{a}}\epsilon^{a[c}\epsilon^{d]b})

(A.2)

However, the same holds for every other balanced bipartition, for instance if we switch the indices
b
and
d

\displaystyle I^{abcd}=\sqrt{\lambda}(e^{i\phi_{s}}\epsilon^{a(c}\epsilon^{d)b}+e^{i\phi_{a}}\epsilon^{a[c}\epsilon^{d]b})=\sqrt{\lambda}(e^{i\psi_{s}}\epsilon^{a(c}\epsilon^{b)d}+e^{i\psi_{a}}\epsilon^{a[c}\epsilon^{b]d}),

(A.3)

which yields constraints on the angles, when calculating the norm of
I

\displaystyle\braket{I|I}=

\displaystyle\lambda(e^{-i\phi_{s}}\epsilon^{a(c}\epsilon^{d)b}+e^{-i\phi_{a}}\epsilon^{a[c}\epsilon^{d]b})(e^{i\psi_{s}}\epsilon_{a(c}\epsilon_{b)d}+e^{i\psi_{a}}\epsilon_{a[c}\epsilon_{b]d})=

(A.4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\left(-\frac{3}{2}e^{i(\psi_{s}-\phi_{s})}-\frac{3}{2}e^{i(\psi_{a}-\phi_{s})}-\frac{3}{2}e^{i(\psi_{s}-\phi_{a})}+\frac{1}{2}e^{i(\psi_{a}-\phi_{a})}\right)

(A.5)

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle\lambda\left(-\frac{3}{2}e^{i\theta_{s}}-\frac{3}{2}e^{i(\theta_{a}+\theta_{s}-\xi)}-\frac{3}{2}e^{i\xi}+\frac{1}{2}e^{i\theta_{a}}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}4\lambda=\braket{I|I},

(A.6)

where we evaluated the inner product of the two sides of (
A.3
) on the left hand side and the norm of
I
on the right hand side using
\braket{I|I}=\Tr(I^{\dagger}I)=\lambda\Tr(\mathds{1})=\lambda(2j+1)^{d}
. We already plugged in the spin quantum number
j=\frac{1}{2}
and the image space dimension
d=\frac{n}{2}=2
. If we split (
A.6
) into real and imaginary part, we can read off the two conditions

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{2}\cos{\theta_{s}}-\frac{3}{2}\cos{(\theta_{a}+\theta_{s}-\xi)}-\frac{3}{2}\cos{i\xi}+\frac{1}{2}\cos{\theta_{a}}

\displaystyle=4

(A.7)

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}\sin{\theta_{s}}+\frac{3}{2}\sin{(\theta_{a}+\theta_{s}-\xi)}+\frac{3}{2}\sin{\xi}-\frac{1}{2}\sin{\theta_{a}}

\displaystyle=0

(A.8)

This equation has infinitely many solutions, for instance
\theta_{a}=\theta_{s}=\xi=\pi
. However, fixing
\xi
also fixes the relative angle between the
\phi_{a/s}
, since
\xi=\psi_{s}-\phi_{a}=\theta_{s}+(\phi_{s}-\phi_{a})
. We can read (
A.7
) as a four-step walk in the complex plane with the destination
4+0i
. To reach the destination, we need the three terms with amplitude
\frac{3}{2}
to be positive and
|\cos(...)|\geq\frac{7}{9}
. This implies the following conditions

\displaystyle\pi-x<\theta_{s}<\pi+x\qquad\pi-x<\theta_{s}-\Delta\phi<\pi+x\qquad\pi-x<\theta_{a}+\Delta\phi<\pi+x

(A.9)

with
x=\cos^{-1}\left(\frac{7}{9}\right)
and
\Delta\phi=\phi_{a}-\phi_{s}
. This implies

\displaystyle\theta_{s}-\pi-x<\Delta\phi<\theta_{s}-\pi+x\qquad\text{ or }\Delta\phi\in[-2x,2x]

(A.10)

If we were to chose a different balanced bipartition, like switching the indices
b
and
c
, the can follow the same approach

\displaystyle I^{abcd}=\sqrt{\lambda}(e^{i\phi_{s}}\epsilon^{a(c}\epsilon^{d)b}+e^{i\phi_{a}}\epsilon^{a[c}\epsilon^{d]b})=\sqrt{\lambda}(e^{i\tilde{\psi}_{s}}\epsilon^{a(b}\epsilon^{d)c}+e^{i\tilde{\psi}_{a}}\epsilon^{a[b}\epsilon^{d]c}),

(A.11)

with new angles
\tilde{\psi}_{a/s}
. The new constraints on the angles, when calculating the norm of
I
are slightly different

\displaystyle\braket{I|I}=

\displaystyle\lambda(e^{-i\phi_{s}}\epsilon^{a(c}\epsilon^{d)b}+e^{-i\phi_{a}}\epsilon^{a[c}\epsilon^{d]b})(e^{i\tilde{\psi}_{s}}\epsilon_{a(b}\epsilon_{d)c}+e^{i\tilde{\psi}_{a}}\epsilon_{a[b}\epsilon_{d]c})=

(A.12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\left(-\frac{3}{2}e^{i(\tilde{\psi}_{s}-\phi_{s})}+\frac{3}{2}e^{i(\tilde{\psi}_{a}-\phi_{s})}+\frac{3}{2}e^{i(\tilde{\psi}_{s}-\phi_{a})}+\frac{1}{2}e^{i(\tilde{\psi}_{a}-\phi_{a})}\right)

(A.13)

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle\lambda\left(-\frac{3}{2}e^{i\tilde{\theta}_{s}}+\frac{3}{2}e^{i(\tilde{\theta}_{a}+\tilde{\theta}_{s}-\tilde{\xi})}+\frac{3}{2}e^{i\tilde{\xi}}+\frac{1}{2}e^{i\tilde{\theta}_{a}}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}4\lambda=\braket{I|I},

(A.14)

We get similar constraints from (
A.14
)

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{2}\cos{\tilde{\theta}_{s}}+\frac{3}{2}\cos{(\tilde{\theta}_{a}+\tilde{\theta}_{s}-\tilde{\xi})}+\frac{3}{2}\cos{i\tilde{\xi}}+\frac{1}{2}\cos{\tilde{\theta}_{a}}

\displaystyle=4

(A.15)

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{2}\sin{\tilde{\theta}_{s}}+\frac{3}{2}\sin{(\tilde{\theta}_{a}+\tilde{\theta}_{s}-\tilde{\xi})}+\frac{3}{2}\sin{\tilde{\xi}}+\frac{1}{2}\sin{\tilde{\theta}_{a}}

\displaystyle=0,

(A.16)

which again put conditions on the angles and consequently on
\Delta\phi

\displaystyle\pi-x<\tilde{\theta}_{s}<\pi+x\qquad-x<\tilde{\theta}_{s}-\Delta\phi<x\qquad-x<\tilde{\theta}_{a}+\Delta\phi<x

(A.17)

\displaystyle\implies\tilde{\theta}_{s}-x<\Delta\phi<\tilde{\theta}_{s}+x\qquad\text{ or }\Delta\phi\in[\pi-2x,\pi+2x]

(A.18)

If we compare this constraint with (
A.10
), we realize a contradiction for the two ranges for
\Delta\phi
have no overlap. This is due to

\displaystyle 2x<\pi-2x\iff x=\cos^{-1}\left(\frac{7}{9}\right)<\frac{\pi}{4}\iff\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}<\frac{7}{9}

(A.19)

which is true. Note that the last equivalence contains a flip of inequality sign due to the monotonously negative slope of the cosine. Analogously,
\pi+2x<2\pi-2x
for the same reason.
∎

Appendix B
Derivation of Master Equations for Invariant Perfect Tensors

In order to formulate a condition for perfectness, we introduce the binor calculus and present a number of propositions that will turn out helpful. Tensor contractions can be depicted using the following basic definitions

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{

(B.1)

Higher spin
j
representations are constructed from the spin
\frac{1}{2}
representation by
2j
symmetrized indices depicted by a rectangle. Every invariant tensor can be decomposed into a contraction of 3-valent invariant tensors. In the binor calculus these are depicted as follows
Kauffman 2002
;
Pietri 1997
.

(B.4)

Proof.

The Kronecker delta
\delta_{j,k}
arises from the fact that tensor must be invariant. Hence, we will assume
j=k
from now on. Both cases
j\pm\frac{1}{2}
can be proven with the binor calculus
Kauffman 2002
. Let us start with the case
j-\frac{1}{2}
. The tensor takes the form

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{
\hbox to63.4pt{\vbox to52.24pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 28.85275pt\lower-36.0556pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -39.37 0 L -19.69 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -19.69 -19.69 M -19.69 -19.69 L -19.69 19.69 L -11.81 19.69 L -11.81 -19.69 Z M -11.81 19.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

(B.6)

where we resolved the symmetrization over
j+\frac{1}{2}
and merged the symmetrization over
j
again, and finally used simple binor identities.
∎

We can use Lemma
B.1
to get a handy expression for the appearance of theta-network structures.

(B.7)

Proof.

This identity is a direct consequence of successively applying Lemma
B.1
, reducing the loops one after another picking up a factor of
\frac{2j_{a-1}+2}{2j_{a-1}+1}
only in the case that
j_{a}=j_{a-1}+\frac{1}{2}
.
∎

Corollary B.3
.

Let
2n\in 2\mathds{N}
and
j_{a},k_{b},\,a,b\in\{1,...,n\}
lists of spin quantum numbers with
j_{n}=k_{n}
. Then

{}_{j}\Theta_{k}=\delta_{j,k}\delta_{j_{1},k_{1}}\prod_{a=2}^{n}\left(\delta_{j_{a},j_{a-1}+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{2j_{a-1}+2}{2j_{a-1}+1}+\delta_{j_{a},j_{a-1}-\frac{1}{2}}\right)(2j_{1}+1)

(B.8)

Proof.

The statement becomes obvious when taking the trace of (
B.7
).
∎

The last ingredient we need is the decomposition of the identity operator into balanced bridge states
\curlyvee_{(j,k)}
. In general, we can decompose any tensor
I
into tensors
\{V_{i}\}
which are orthogonal with respect to the Hilbert Schmidt product

\displaystyle\braket{V_{i}|V_{j}}=\delta_{i,j}||V_{i}||^{2}.

(B.9)

In (
B.9
), the tensors
V_{i}
do not need to be normalized. We will show now that the bridge states as defined in Definition
IV.1
satisfy these orthogonality relations.

Lemma B.4
.

Let
\curlyvee_{(j,k)}
and
\curlyvee_{(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})}
be two bridge states as defined in Definition
IV.1
. The bridge states are orthogonal, i.e.

\displaystyle\braket{\curlyvee_{(j,k)}|\curlyvee_{(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})}}=\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}\delta_{k,k^{\prime}}||\curlyvee_{(j,k)}||^{2}

(B.10)

with the norm being just a theta network as defined in Definition
IV.3

\displaystyle||\curlyvee_{(j,k)}||^{2}=_{(j,k)}\Theta_{(j,k)}

(B.11)

Proof.

\displaystyle\braket{\curlyvee_{(j,k)}|\curlyvee_{(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})}}=\Tr\left(\curlyvee^{\dagger}_{(j,k)}\curlyvee_{(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})}\right)=\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{

}}}}}}=_{(j,k)}\Theta_{(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})}=\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}\delta_{k,k^{\prime}}{}_{(j,k)}\Theta_{(j,k)},

(B.12)

where we indicated index contractions by dotted lines and the last step uses the orthogonality of theta networks from Corollary
B.3
.
∎

Remark B.5
.

As every bridge state is also a coupling scheme into Wigner 3j symbols
Wigner 1993
the set of all invariant
n
-valent bridge states is also a complete basis.

The decomposition of the identity operator into bridge states will be of central importance in the following discussion.

Lemma B.6
.

Let
\curlyvee_{(j,k)}
be balanced (
n_{1}=n_{2}=n
) bridge states as defined in Definition
IV.1
. The bridge state decomposition of the identity reads

\displaystyle\mathds{1}=\sum_{j,k}\frac{{}_{j}\Theta_{k}}{{}_{(j,k)}\Theta_{(j,k)}}\curlyvee_{(j,k)}

(B.13)

Proof.

Since the bridge states
\{\curlyvee_{(j,k)}\}
form a basis of the invariant tensors, we can write the identity as a linear combination

\displaystyle\mathds{1}=\sum_{j,k}c_{j,k}\curlyvee_{(j,k)}

(B.14)

The coefficients
c_{j,k}
can be found by calculating the inner products

\displaystyle c_{j,k}=\frac{\braket{\curlyvee_{(j,k)}|\mathds{1}}}{||\curlyvee_{(j,k)}||^{2}}=\frac{1}{{}_{(j,k)}\Theta_{(j,k)}}\Tr(\curlyvee^{\dagger}_{(j,k)})=\frac{{}_{j}\Theta_{k}}{{}_{(j,k)}\Theta_{(j,k)}}

(B.15)

which follows from the orthogonality of the bridge states
\curlyvee_{(j,k)}
.
∎

With the preparations made, we can now precisely formulate conditions for invariant perfect tensors.

Proof of Theorem
IV.4
.

We proceed by calculating both sides of (
IV.3
) in the bridge state decomposition. As we already know the bridge state decomposition of
\mathds{1}
from Lemma
B.6
, we are left with calculating the left hand side:

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k,j^{\prime}}c_{j,k}\overline{c_{j^{\prime},k}}\frac{{}_{k}\Theta_{k}}{2k_{n_{2}}+1}\curlyvee_{(j,j^{\prime})}

(B.16)

For the right hand side of (
IV.3
) let us recall the bridge decomposition of
\mathds{1}

\displaystyle\mathds{1}=\sum_{j,j^{\prime}}\frac{{}_{j}\Theta_{j^{\prime}}}{{}_{(j,j^{\prime})}\Theta_{(j,j^{\prime})}}\curlyvee_{(j,j^{\prime})}

(B.17)

These coefficients can be compared to (
B.16
) yielding the equations

\displaystyle\sum_{k}c_{j,k}\overline{c_{j^{\prime},k}}\frac{{}_{k}\Theta_{k}}{2k_{n_{2}}+1}=\frac{{}_{j}\Theta_{j^{\prime}}}{{}_{(j,j^{\prime})}\Theta_{(j,j^{\prime})}}

(B.18)

Let us further inspect the right hand side of this equation to get rid of the double evaluation of theta networks

\displaystyle\frac{{}_{j}\Theta_{j^{\prime}}}{{}_{(j,j^{\prime})}\Theta_{(j,j^{\prime})}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}\frac{\prod_{a=1}^{n_{1}-1}\left(\delta_{j_{a},j_{a-1}+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{2j_{a-1}+2}{2j_{a-1}+1}+\delta_{j_{a},j_{a-1}-\frac{1}{2}}\right)(2j_{n_{1}}+1)}{\prod_{a=1}^{n_{1}-1}\left(\delta_{j_{a},j_{a-1}+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{2j_{a-1}+2}{2j_{a-1}+1}+\delta_{j_{a},j_{a-1}-\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{2}(2j_{n_{1}}+1)}

\displaystyle=\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\prod_{a=1}^{n_{1}-1}\left(\delta_{j_{a},j_{a-1}+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{2j_{a-1}+2}{2j_{a-1}+1}+\delta_{j_{a},j_{a-1}-\frac{1}{2}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}\frac{2j_{n_{1}}+1}{{}_{j}\Theta_{j}}

(B.19)

Finally, multiplying (
B.18
) with
2j_{n_{1}}+1
and using
j_{n_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}k_{n_{2}}
yields the result.
∎

Remark B.7
.

Changing the number of indices to odd valence is straightforward in the above discussion. The crucial difference comes from the fact that no invariant spin
\frac{1}{2}
encodings with odd valence exist. Thus, the discussion needs to be generalized to higher spins
l\geq\frac{1}{2}
.

To do this, Lemma
B.1
needs to be generalized for higher excitations. The condition
\delta_{j,k}
remains the same, but the spin numbers in the middle loop of (
B.4
) become
l
and
\tilde{l}\in\{|j-l|,|j-l|+1,...,j+l\}
. The multiplicative factor on the right hand side of (
B.4
) will depend on
\tilde{l}
. This makes the generalization of Theorem
IV.4
to arbitrary spin
l
straightforward, but tedious for high spin
l
, as every combination of
l_{1},l_{2}\in\{|j-l|,|j-l|+1,...,j+l\}
has to be considered in the basis state decompositions.

The smallest non-trivial odd-valent candidate of an IPT, for instance, would be the five-valent tensor with all indices being
j=1
representations. In the bridge state expansion, it would have the form

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k}c_{j,k}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{

(B.20)

with seven coefficients (two more than the six-valent spin
\frac{1}{2}
candidate, see Appendix
E
).

Appendix C
Additional Equations from Repartition

Before we study applications of (
IV.5
), we want to present a number of repartitions which reduce the further analysis to only non-trivial permutations. We further sketch a systematic method of how to translate non-trivial repartitions into additional constraint equations for the coefficients
c_{(j,k)}
.

C.1
Trivial Repartitions

Even though the previous discussion holds for arbitrary bipartitions with index set
|A|\leq|B|
, we will show that only the balanced (or almost balanced in the case of odd
n
) bipartitions are of relevance for the study of invariant perfect tensors.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
I
be an invariant tensor that satisfies
I^{\dagger}I=\lambda\mathds{1}
for some
\lambda\in\mathds{R}
and an implicit bipartition
A,B
. Then, the tensor
\tilde{I}
which emerges from
I
by moving one index from
A
into
B
is also a partial isometry
\tilde{I}^{\dagger}\tilde{I}=\tilde{\lambda}\mathds{1}
.

Proof.

Let us write the tensor
I
in index notation
\mathchoice{I^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.26047pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.82945pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A_{1}...A_{a}}}}_{{{B_{1}...B_{b}}\mathchoice{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.0334pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.7667pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{I^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.26047pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.82945pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A_{1}...A_{a}}}}_{{{B_{1}...B_{b}}\mathchoice{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.0334pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.7667pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{I^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.26047pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.82945pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A_{1}...A_{a}}}}_{{{B_{1}...B_{b}}\mathchoice{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.0334pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.7667pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{I^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.26047pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.82945pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A_{1}...A_{a}}}}_{{{B_{1}...B_{b}}\mathchoice{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.0334pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.7667pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
. It satisfies

\displaystyle\mathchoice{I^{{{\dagger}\mathchoice{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.0334pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.7667pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B_{1}...B_{b}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.66667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.66667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17778pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55556pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A_{1}...A_{a}}\mathchoice{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.26047pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.82945pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{I^{{{\dagger}\mathchoice{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.0334pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.7667pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B_{1}...B_{b}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.66667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.66667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17778pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55556pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A_{1}...A_{a}}\mathchoice{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.26047pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.82945pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{I^{{{\dagger}\mathchoice{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.0334pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.7667pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B_{1}...B_{b}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.66667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.66667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17778pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55556pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A_{1}...A_{a}}\mathchoice{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.26047pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.82945pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{I^{{{\dagger}\mathchoice{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.0334pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.7667pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B_{1}...B_{b}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.66667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.66667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17778pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55556pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A_{1}...A_{a}}\mathchoice{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.26047pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.82945pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathchoice{I^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.26047pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.82945pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A^{\prime}_{1}...A^{\prime}_{a}}}}_{{{B_{1}...B_{b}}\mathchoice{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.0334pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.7667pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{I^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.26047pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.82945pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A^{\prime}_{1}...A^{\prime}_{a}}}}_{{{B_{1}...B_{b}}\mathchoice{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.0334pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.7667pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{I^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.26047pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.82945pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A^{\prime}_{1}...A^{\prime}_{a}}}}_{{{B_{1}...B_{b}}\mathchoice{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.0334pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.7667pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{I^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.26047pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.82945pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A^{\prime}_{1}...A^{\prime}_{a}}}}_{{{B_{1}...B_{b}}\mathchoice{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[28.95683pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.0334pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.7667pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=\lambda\delta^{A^{\prime}_{1}}_{A_{1}}\cdots\delta^{A^{\prime}_{a}}_{A_{a}}

(C.1)

Assume without loss of generality that we move the
a^{\text{th}}
index to
B
. The new tensor satisfies

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\tilde{I}^{{{\dagger}\mathchoice{\makebox[33.04016pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[33.04016pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[18.95006pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[15.68335pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A_{a}B_{1}...B_{b}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.66667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.66667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17778pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55556pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A_{1}...A_{a-1}}\mathchoice{\makebox[38.31181pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[38.31181pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[22.28552pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[17.8045pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\tilde{I}^{{{\dagger}\mathchoice{\makebox[33.04016pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[33.04016pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[18.95006pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[15.68335pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A_{a}B_{1}...B_{b}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.66667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.66667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17778pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55556pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A_{1}...A_{a-1}}\mathchoice{\makebox[38.31181pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[38.31181pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[22.28552pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[17.8045pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\tilde{I}^{{{\dagger}\mathchoice{\makebox[33.04016pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[33.04016pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[18.95006pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[15.68335pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A_{a}B_{1}...B_{b}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.66667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.66667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17778pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55556pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A_{1}...A_{a-1}}\mathchoice{\makebox[38.31181pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[38.31181pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[22.28552pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[17.8045pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\tilde{I}^{{{\dagger}\mathchoice{\makebox[33.04016pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[33.04016pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[18.95006pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[15.68335pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A_{a}B_{1}...B_{b}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.66667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.66667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17778pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55556pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A_{1}...A_{a-1}}\mathchoice{\makebox[38.31181pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[38.31181pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[22.28552pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[17.8045pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathchoice{I^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.26047pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.82945pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A^{\prime}_{1}...A^{\prime}_{a-1}}\mathchoice{\makebox[9.104pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.104pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.02505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.97505pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{B_{1}...B_{b}}\mathchoice{\makebox[33.04016pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[33.04016pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[18.95006pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[15.68335pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A_{a}}}}}{I^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.26047pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.82945pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A^{\prime}_{1}...A^{\prime}_{a-1}}\mathchoice{\makebox[9.104pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.104pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.02505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.97505pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{B_{1}...B_{b}}\mathchoice{\makebox[33.04016pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[33.04016pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[18.95006pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[15.68335pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A_{a}}}}}{I^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.26047pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.82945pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A^{\prime}_{1}...A^{\prime}_{a-1}}\mathchoice{\makebox[9.104pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.104pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.02505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.97505pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{B_{1}...B_{b}}\mathchoice{\makebox[33.04016pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[33.04016pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[18.95006pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[15.68335pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A_{a}}}}}{I^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[29.20781pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.26047pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.82945pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A^{\prime}_{1}...A^{\prime}_{a-1}}\mathchoice{\makebox[9.104pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.104pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.02505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.97505pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{B_{1}...B_{b}}\mathchoice{\makebox[33.04016pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[33.04016pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[18.95006pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[15.68335pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A_{a}}}}}=\lambda\delta^{A^{\prime}_{1}}_{A_{1}}\cdots\delta^{A^{\prime}_{a-1}}_{A_{a-1}}\delta^{A_{a}}_{A_{a}}=(2j_{a}+1)\lambda\delta^{A^{\prime}_{1}}_{A_{1}}\cdots\delta^{A^{\prime}_{a-1}}_{A_{a-1}}.

(C.2)

Which proves the statement with a new scaling factor
\tilde{\lambda}=(2j_{a}+1)\lambda
where
j_{a}
denotes the spin quantum number corresponding to the
a^{\text{th}}
index.
∎

As a consequence of Lemma
C.1
, we will, without loss of generality, focus on balanced bipartitions in the following. Another set of trivial repartitions is the permutation of neighboring indices within the domain
A
or within the image
B
.

Lemma C.2
.

Let
I
be an invariant tensor that satisfies
I^{\dagger}I=\lambda\mathds{1}
for some
\lambda\in\mathds{R}
and an implicit bipartition
A,B
. Then the following tensors are also isometries

1.

\tilde{I}
emerging from
I
by exchanging two domain indices
A_{i},A_{j}\in A

2.

\tilde{I}
emerging from
I
by exchanging two image indices
B_{i},B_{j}\in B

Proof.

The proposition can be seen in the binor formulation of the isometry property. We know that
I
satisfies

}}}}}}$}.

(C.5)

For the exchange of non-neighboring indices, the above calculations can be iterated.
∎

C.2
Non-Trivial Repartitions

Besides the exchange of neighboring indices on the same side of the bipartition, we are left with one last repartition from nearest-neighbor permutations. This last repartition, however, has a non-trivial effect on the solution of (
IV.5
), i.e. additional equations arise if we require the repartitioned tensor to also be a partial isometry. Before we study this last repartition, we point out a helpful detail arising from the fact that neighboring permutations are involutions

Lemma C.3
.

Let
I
be an invariant tensor that satisfies
I^{\dagger}I=\lambda\mathds{1}
for some
\lambda\in\mathds{R}
and an implicit bipartition
A,B
. Let further
\tilde{I}
be a repartition of
I
such that

\displaystyle\tilde{I}=\sum_{j,k}\tilde{c}_{j,k}\curlyvee_{j,k}=\sum_{j,k,j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\mathchoice{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.33281pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.33281pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.14243pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.37949pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}}}_{{{j,k}\mathchoice{\makebox[15.45834pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.45834pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.28964pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.47115pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.33281pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.33281pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.14243pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.37949pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}}}_{{{j,k}\mathchoice{\makebox[15.45834pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.45834pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.28964pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.47115pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.33281pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.33281pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.14243pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.37949pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}}}_{{{j,k}\mathchoice{\makebox[15.45834pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.45834pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.28964pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.47115pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.33281pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.33281pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.14243pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.37949pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}}}_{{{j,k}\mathchoice{\makebox[15.45834pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.45834pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.28964pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.47115pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}c_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\curlyvee_{j,k}

(C.6)

with some involution
P
that satisfies
P^{2}=\mathds{1}
. If
\tilde{I}
is a partial symmetry with
\tilde{I}^{\dagger}\tilde{I}=\tilde{\lambda}\mathds{1}
, then
\lambda=\tilde{\lambda}
.

Proof.

For the sake of clarity, we introduce a combined index
\mu:=(j,k)
, such that

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{\mu}\curlyvee^{\mu}=\mathchoice{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}c_{\nu}\curlyvee^{\mu}

(C.7)

Since
P
is an involution, its eigenvalues are
\pm 1
. We can thus describe the action of
P
on its normalized eigenvectors
\ket{p^{i}}=\mathchoice{d^{{{-1}{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.50157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.50157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.08333pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.91666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{d^{{{-1}{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.50157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.50157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.08333pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.91666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{d^{{{-1}{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.50157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.50157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.08333pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.91666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{d^{{{-1}{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.50157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.50157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.08333pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.91666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}\curlyvee^{\mu}

\displaystyle P\left(\sum_{\mu}c_{\mu}||\curlyvee^{\mu}||\frac{\curlyvee^{\mu}}{||\curlyvee^{\mu}||}\right)=P\left(\sum_{\mu,i}c_{\mu}||\curlyvee^{\mu}||\mathchoice{d^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{d^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{d^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{d^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}\ket{p^{i}}\right)=:P(a_{i}\ket{p^{i}})=\sum_{i}a_{i}(-1)^{x_{p_{i}}}\ket{p^{i}}

(C.8)

where
x_{p_{i}}\in\{0,1\}
and denotes whether the
i^{th}
eigenvalue is
+1
or
-1
. To prove the statement, we compare the norms of
I
and
\tilde{I}
. From the partial isometry property, we know that
\braket{I|I}=\Tr(I^{\dagger}I)=\Tr(\lambda\mathds{1})=\lambda d
with
d
being the dimension of the state space on which
I^{\dagger}I
is acting. Analogously, we get
\braket{\tilde{I}|\tilde{I}}=\tilde{\lambda}d
. We can relate the norm of
\tilde{I}
to
\lambda
using (
C.8
)

\displaystyle\braket{I|I}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu}|c_{\mu}|^{2}||\curlyvee^{\mu}||^{2}=\sum_{i}|a_{i}|^{2}

(C.9)

\displaystyle\braket{\tilde{I}|\tilde{I}}

\displaystyle=\Tr\left(P\left(\sum_{\mu}c_{\mu}||\curlyvee^{\mu}||\frac{\curlyvee^{\mu}}{||\curlyvee^{\mu}||}\right)^{\dagger}P\left(\sum_{\mu}c_{\mu}||\curlyvee^{\mu}||\frac{\curlyvee^{\mu}}{||\curlyvee^{\mu}||}\right)\right)=\sum_{i}|a_{i}(-1)^{x_{p_{i}}}|^{2}=\sum_{i}|a_{i}|^{2}

(C.10)

∎

This implies
\tilde{\lambda}=\lambda
.

Lemma C.4
.

Let
I
be an invariant tensor that satisfies
I^{\dagger}I=\lambda\mathds{1}
for some
\lambda\in\mathds{R}
and an implicit bipartition
A,B

(C.13)

with
\tilde{\lambda}\in\mathds{R}
and where we used the binor identity to get rid of the crossings. From Lemma
C.3
we know that
\lambda=\tilde{\lambda}
. As we know that
I
is a partial isometry, we can cancel the first term of the left hand side with the right hand side of (
C.13
) and deduce the statement.
∎

Note that we necessarily pull indices with the Levi-Civita tensor
\epsilon
in order to use the binor identity in (
C.13
). It can be straight-forwardly shown that the isometry properties of
I
and
\epsilon I\epsilon^{\dagger}
are equivalent which allows us to do so.

In general, the condition (
C.12
) turns out to be difficult to further break down in general. However, it can always be evaluated by decomposing the remaining terms again into bridge states. The equation (
C.12
) might put new constraints on the previously found coefficients. This procedure needs to be iterated over all combinations of trivial permutations
P_{i,j}
preceding the critical permutation
P_{n,n+1}
. Since they do not commute, in general, new constraints might arise for the coefficients
c_{j,k}
. We illustrate this method on two examples in appendices
D
and
E
, respectively.

Appendix D
No 4-valent invariant perfect tensors – Proof II

Second Proof of Theorem
III.5
.

We proceed by contradiction. Let us assume there would exist a 4-valent invariant perfect tensor
I
with spin
\frac{1}{2}
. The bridge decomposition of
I
reads

\displaystyle I=\sum_{j,k}c_{j,k}\curlyvee_{(j,k)}

(D.1)

For spin
\frac{1}{2}
and a fixed bipartition, there are only two basis states with
j=k=\left(\frac{1}{2},0\right)
and
j=k=\left(\frac{1}{2},1\right)
, which we will denote
\curlyvee_{0}
and
\curlyvee_{1}
. By Theorem
IV.4
, the coefficients have to satisfy the set of equations

\displaystyle|c_{1}|^{2}=\lambda\qquad

\displaystyle\iff\qquad c_{1}=\sqrt{\lambda}e^{i\phi_{1}}

(D.2)

\displaystyle|c_{0}|^{2}=\frac{\lambda}{4}\qquad

\displaystyle\iff\qquad c_{0}=\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}}{2}e^{i\phi_{0}}.

(D.3)

If we do a repartition changing the neighboring indices across the bridge, we map
I
to
\tilde{I}
which we can write as

\displaystyle=c_{0}\left(\curlyvee_{0}-\mathds{1}\right)+c_{1}\left(\curlyvee_{1}-\frac{3}{2}\mathds{1}\right)

(D.4)

We can insert the bridge state decomposition of
\mathds{1}=\frac{1}{2}\curlyvee_{0}+\curlyvee_{1}
(cf. Lemma
B.6
) to read off the new coefficients
\tilde{c}_{j}

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{0}=\frac{1}{2}c_{0}-\frac{3}{4}c_{1}\qquad\qquad\tilde{c}_{1}=-c_{0}-\frac{1}{2}c_{1}

(D.5)

where we used that
{}_{(\frac{1}{2},1)}\Theta_{(\frac{1}{2},1)}=_{(\frac{1}{2},1,\frac{1}{2})}\Theta_{(\frac{1}{2},1,\frac{1}{2})}=3
,
{}_{(\frac{1}{2},0)}\Theta_{(\frac{1}{2},0)}=2
and
{}_{(\frac{1}{2},0,\frac{1}{2})}\Theta_{(\frac{1}{2},0,\frac{1}{2})}=4
. The twiddled coefficients
\tilde{c}_{j}
have to satisfy the same equations as before

\displaystyle|\tilde{c}_{1}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\lambda}=|c_{0}|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}|c_{1}|^{2}+\Real(\overline{c_{0}}c_{1})=\frac{\lambda}{2}\left(1+\cos(\phi_{1}-\phi_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\iff\frac{\tilde{\lambda}}{\lambda}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\cos(\phi_{1}-\phi_{0})

(D.6)

\displaystyle|\tilde{c}_{0}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\lambda}}{4}=\frac{1}{4}|c_{0}|^{2}+\frac{9}{16}|c_{1}|^{2}-\frac{3}{4}\Real(\overline{c_{0}}c_{1})=\frac{\lambda}{8}\left(5-3\cos(\phi_{1}-\phi_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\iff\frac{\tilde{\lambda}}{\lambda}=\frac{5}{2}-\frac{3}{2}\cos(\phi_{1}-\phi_{0}),

(D.7)

With the equations (
D.6
) and (
D.7
), we can solve for
\frac{\tilde{\lambda}}{\lambda}
as well as
\Delta\phi:=\phi_{1}-\phi_{0}

\displaystyle 1+\cos(\Delta\phi)=5-3\cos(\Delta\phi)\qquad\iff\qquad\Delta\phi=0\mod 2\pi

(D.8)

which implies
\frac{\tilde{\lambda}}{\lambda}=1
and is consistent with Lemma
C.3
. Lastly, we consider a repartition that swaps the two indices on the right of bridge state of
I
, then performs the repartition as in (
D.4
) and finally swaps the two right indices again. Since the neighboring permutations do not commute, we get a non-trivial new repartition
\tilde{\tilde{I}}
via

\displaystyle\tilde{\tilde{I}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\{0,1\}}\tilde{\tilde{c_{j}}}\curlyvee_{j}=\sum_{j\in\{0,1\}}c_{j}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{
\hbox to130.33pt{\vbox to24.37pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 16.11421pt\lower-9.74484pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 19.69 L 19.69 19.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
